# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

1. Description of Dataset and Learning Task
1.1 Source, records and suitability
BattLeDIM is an open, simulated drinking-water benchmark representing L-Town, inspired by Cyprus. It was created to compare
leak detection/localization methods. Its continuous time series also allow a supervised pressure-forecasting task. Zenodo supplies
supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) records, leak labels, sensor configuration and EPANET hydraulic models [1,2].
These are simulated observations, not field measurements.
The 2018-2019 release has five-minute spacing: 105,120 timestamps per year, 210,240 total, and 119 numerical measurement
channels per timestamp. The nominal network contains 782 demand junctions, two reservoirs, one tank, 905 pipes, one pump and
three pressure-reducing valves (PRVs). Nominal and generator parameters differ [1].
Quantity
Channels / units
Meaning
Pressure
33; m water head
Pressure at measured junctions.
Pipe/pump flow
3; m3/h
PUMP_1 and p227/p235 flow.
Tank level
1; m
Water level in T1.
AMR demand
82; L/h
Automated meter reading (AMR): consumption assigned to junctions, not pipe
inflow.
Our checks of all eight annual SCADA tables found matching, unique timestamps and no missing values. The benchmark assumes
error-free sensors and no transmission delay/loss. Values are rounded to two decimals; pressure readings average the preceding five
minutes [2], Sensors and Telemetry. Nearby time records are related, so the later evaluation keeps time order. Leak labels are not
model inputs.
1.2 Learning task and hydraulic feature selection
The task is single-output supervised regression: y(u)=p_n1(u+15 min), three samples ahead, in metres. The label is the recorded
five-minute average ending at u+15, including the simulated operating/leak conditions. Each input row is paired with n1 three
timestamps later; there are no target classes. The intended use is a short look-ahead for operators reviewing pressure changes at a
remote junction; operational benefits are not evaluated here. Every input must be available at forecast time u.
Figure 1. Functional supply boundary derived from the released model [1]. Nominal PRV settings are 40/50/35 m; constant settings do not fix
remote-node pressures.
Area C is supplied through T1 and has 92 junctions, three pressure sensors and 82 AMRs. Our nominal-model checks give n1
the longest T1-to-sensor pipe path: 891 m, versus 574/308 m for n4/n31; this distance is not a signal delay. We keep n1/n4/n31
pressure, T1 level, PUMP_1 flow and AMR_n1 demand: 119 channels →87 local/boundary candidates →six signals. T1
represents storage, PUMP_1 measured inflow, n4/n31 the local pressure state, and AMR_n1 consumption at the target junction.
Unlike PRV-3’s single measured pressure, C provides several related measurements. Whether they help n1 forecasting is a question
to test.

1.3 Own exploratory analysis
Our exploratory data analysis (EDA) uses January-September 2018 only: 273 days and 78,624 timestamps. n1 ranges from 25.67
to 29.36 m. The generator opens PUMP_1 below T1=2.4 m and closes it above 3.9 m [1]. Figure 2 shows how pressure, tank level
and pump operation vary through the day; the mean curve does not describe every individual day.
Figure 2. Own EDA: T1/PUMP_1 at left; n1/n4/n31 at right. Thin lines: individual days; red: clock-time mean.
For 78,621 valid +15 pairs, excluding the final three origins, persistence predicts the current n1 value. Its mean absolute error
(MAE) is 0.0518 m (about 5.18 cm); root mean squared error (RMSE) is 0.0685 m and gives more weight to large errors. These
are our 2018 descriptive results, not final test scores. Correlation with future n1 is 0.9923 for current n1, 0.9230 for T1, -0.0367
for PUMP_1 and -0.1574 for AMR_n1. Pressure changes slowly over 15 minutes, making persistence a strong baseline; high
correlation alone does not show that an extra signal improves a forecast.
Figure 3. Own EDA: six origin inputs and the future label. The label is separated for analysis and never enters X.
2. Contributions of Previous Authors
We searched BattLeDIM/L-Town predictive models and followed citations/code. Related papers/repositories form 35 groups of
related works on L-Town or explicit modifications; external networks, reviews and tools were excluded. The five categories mean
leak detection/location, current-pressure estimation, future-pressure/head forecasting, anomaly/sensor-fault detection, and sensor
placement. This inventory is not exhaustive.

ChebNet, SAM-kNN and AGN are core future-state studies; LILA/GATRes are current-state comparators; EILF is supporting
hydraulic engineering. SAM-kNN/AGN/GATRes generate new L-Town data rather than using the released SCADA. Training was

Data: Leak-free nominal simulations, network links and
three past pressure readings; 80/20 train/validation split.
Models: Two four-layer graph networks: one forecasts
healthy pressure (+5 min), the other reconstructs current
pressure from available sensors. Their discrepancy over
seven days helps locate leaks. Graph/scaling options were
compared. Code fits scaling before the split.
2019 diagnosis, Table 1: 19 accepted matches,
138 false alarms, four missed leaks; recall 82.61%,
EUR326,521. A match requires a location within 300
m while the leak is active. Many false alarms remain;
these results do not measure forecast-pressure error.

virtual pressure;

Data: Ten new Area A simulations (3 months each; 29

paper describes averaging four. Model: Adaptive
nearest-neighbor regression stores recent/older examples
and selects the memory with the lowest tracked prediction

Five leak cases, Table 1: Each leak is detected,
with 3-48 false alarms per scenario versus

Data: One generated year on modified L-Town: 5-min
samples, 104 demand patterns; 70/20/10
train/validation/test split. Elevation and water-use
analysis guides features. Model: Graph links share
information; recurrent units retain history; attention
weights nodes/features to forecast head (pressure head +
elevation). Training progressively hides fewer inputs.
Future heads, Table 1: 12/24/48/96-step outputs
reach +1/+2/+4/+8 h. For the 12-step window,
regional MAE is 0.859 m (high), 0.327 m (medium),
32.7 m (low elevation). Errors average whole
windows/regions; +15 is included, but no separate
n1(+15) score is reported.

healthy-pressure

[9].
Data: Released SCADA; pressure/flow analysis guides
reference-sensor selection. Leak-free calibration periods
and some per-leak node combinations are manually
selected. Model: Linear regression estimates current
healthy pressure from peer pressures/flow. CUSUM adds
persistent deviations to detect leaks, then hydraulic
calculations locate them. Paper: squared flow; code: raw
PUMP_1. It is not a future forecast.
Journal Table 1: 19 new 2019 leaks; four inherited
leaks excluded. TP=17, FP=2, FN=0; precision
89.5%, F1=0.944, EUR307,852. Two locations
outside 300 m count as false positives, not missed

Data: 10,000 simulated L-Town snapshots (60/20/20
train/validation/test split); compare pressure

selected readings and fill hidden inputs with zero. Graph
attention weights neighboring information to reconstruct
the hidden pressures at the same time point.

Data: 2018 development/2019 test. In Area C, pump
inflow minus the tank-storage change rate estimates sector
outflow; summed AMRs provide a reference. Use 30-min
windows to reduce level-rounding effects. Method:
Separate pressure trends from recurring cycles (STL),
detect sustained changes, then locate leaks hydraulically.
Journal conclusion, p. 11: TPR=47.83%, five
false positives, EUR167,981. These measure leak
diagnosis, not future-pressure accuracy. The
tank-balance calculation is an existing engineering
contribution relevant to our feature selection.
TP: correctly identified leaks; FP: false alarms/incorrect matches; FN: missed leaks. TPR: recall; F1: balance between precision
and recall. EUR: competition monetary score [2].
Research gap. Among the reviewed studies, none reports a directly comparable n1(+15) forecast evaluation on the released
SCADA. Leak diagnosis, current reconstruction and forecasts with other data/horizons answer different questions. We therefore
compare Gradient Boosting, persistence, Ridge and Random Forest on common chronological splits and test auxiliary-signal value.
This provides reproducible classical baselines for future comparison with complex models, which are not reproduced here.

3. Your Anticipated Contributions
3.1 Proposed contribution
We propose to forecast recorded n1 pressure 15 minutes ahead using Gradient Boosting and the six signals selected in Section 1:
n1/n4/n31 pressures, T1 level, PUMP_1 flow and AMR_n1 demand. The contribution would be a reproducible comparison for
this specific task using the released SCADA data.
The reviewed graph and adaptive-kNN studies address related prediction tasks. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions
[13,14], and Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees without specifying a future lead [15]. Our review did not identify this
exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. We therefore propose an extension of the reviewed applications, without claiming a new
algorithm or worldwide priority.
Planned contribution
Question to answer
C1. Prepared forecast data
Can we organize past measurements and their +15-minute pressure labels without using future
inputs?
C2. Model comparison
Does Gradient Boosting improve on persistence, Ridge and Random Forest?
C3. Value of extra signals
Do the tank, pump, peer pressures and local demand help beyond n1 history?
3.2 Data preparation and models
We will align timestamps, check units and missing values, and pair each input row with n1 three readings later. Starting inputs will
include current readings and the previous 55 minutes at five-minute intervals, plus time of day and day of week. Only information
available at forecast time will be used; leak labels will stay outside the model inputs. Rows without required values or with a
target outside their assigned period will be excluded. Any scaling for Ridge will be fitted on training data only. Section 1 EDA
motivates these choices but does not establish that every signal helps.
Gradient Boosting adds small decision trees that try to correct earlier prediction errors [18]. We will compare it with persistence (use
the current n1 pressure), Ridge (a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients) [16], and Random Forest (average predictions
from many trees) [17]. These models build on regression and ensemble ideas studied in class. Model settings will be chosen using
validation data; detailed parameter choices are left for implementation.
3.3 Evaluation and expected improvement
We will train on January-September 2018, use October-December 2018 to select settings, then refit on eligible 2018 rows and assess
2019. This preserves time order. Since 2019 has already been inspected, it is a final chronological assessment. All models will
predict the same rows.
We will report MAE (average absolute error) and RMSE (which gives more weight to large errors), both in metres. A boosting
advantage would require lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. To test the extra signals, we will also compare
full boosting with boosting using n1 history and the same calendar variables. Extra-signal value requires lower MAE than this
reduced model and persistence. We will report RMSE tradeoffs and cases where no improvement is found.
The expected outputs are an annotated preparation/model notebook, a comparison table and forecast/error plots. These are
planned experiments; no learning model has been fitted.

DOCUMENT Milestone1.pdf PAGE 1 
Mini-Project Milestone 1
Proposed Datasets for Pressure Prediction
DS675
Andres Arango Martinez
NJIT ID: 31748996
|
UCID: aa3978
Area of interest and selection rationale.
My research interests include pressure management in drinking-water
distribution systems, particularly pressure behavior at remote critical nodes. I have previously worked on this problem using
field measurements and model-based control methods, as described in a published case study. For this mini-project, I would
like to study whether supervised machine learning can use current and historical SCADA measurements to forecast future
pressure at a monitored node. The assignment asks for Kaggle datasets with usability above 9. BATADAL satisfies that
criterion with a Kaggle usability of 10.0. BattLeDIM currently shows 3.53 on Kaggle, so it does not meet that specific
threshold; I retain it as a candidate because its original source has substantial academic support, including a peer-reviewed
ASCE benchmark paper, an official research archive, and EPANET network models.

Candidate 1
BattLeDIM: L-Town
Dataset characteristics and academic provenance. BattLeDIM is a simulated SCADA benchmark for L-Town, a
city-scale drinking-water network inspired by a real system in Cyprus. Its Kaggle copy has a usability rating of 3.53, which
is below the assignment’s requested value of greater than 9. I am including it transparently because the underlying dataset
is not an informal Kaggle-only collection: it was developed for an international leakage-detection benchmark, documented
in a peer-reviewed Journal of Water Resources Planning and Management paper, and released with an official Zenodo
archive and EPANET network models. The benchmark paper describes 782 junctions and 905 pipe segments and divides the
network into three hydraulic areas. The SCADA data contain 105,120 five-minute observations in each of 2018 and 2019, for
210,240 observations total. The main measurement table has 119 variables: 33 pressures, three flows, one tank level, and
82 automated meter reading (AMR) demand measurements. Leakage-event information is also available from the original
benchmark for later diagnostic analysis.
Proposed predictive-learning task. I would use BattLeDIM for supervised regression: predict the recorded pressure
head at one monitored node 15 minutes into the future. Predictors would be limited to information available at forecast
time, such as current and lagged pressures, flows, tank level, AMR demands, and calendar features. I would select the
target sensor after exploratory analysis of topology and pressure variability rather than labeling a node “critical” in advance.
Because 119 candidate variables may be unnecessary, I would compare an all-network baseline with a topology-informed
subset drawn from the same hydraulic area and its boundary connections. Leakage periods could later be used to evaluate
whether forecast errors change under abnormal hydraulic conditions.
Sources: Kaggle
| Official Zenodo archive
| Vrachimis et al. (2022), ASCE


DOCUMENT Milestone1.pdf PAGE 2 
Mini-Project Milestone 1
Proposed Datasets for Pressure Prediction
DS675
Andres Arango Martinez
Candidate 2
Candidate 2
BATADAL: C-Town
Dataset characteristics and academic provenance. BATADAL is a simulated SCADA benchmark created for the
Battle of the Attack Detection Algorithms on the C-Town water-distribution system. Its Kaggle copy has a usability rating
of 10.0, satisfying the assignment requirement. Like BattLeDIM, it also has strong support beyond Kaggle: the original
challenge provides official datasets, attack-event information, the C-Town EPANET model and control materials, and the
benchmark was documented in a peer-reviewed ASCE paper. C-Town is a medium-sized network with 388 junctions, 429
pipes, seven tanks, 11 pumps, five valves, nine programmable logic controllers, and a SCADA system. The released data are
sampled hourly and contain 43 process variables: seven tank levels, 12 pressures, 12 flows (11 pumps plus one actuated valve),
and 12 binary actuator states. The three principal files contain 8,761 normal-operation records, 4,177 mixed development
records, and 2,089 test records, for 15,027 observations total.
Proposed predictive-learning task. I would use BATADAL for supervised regression: predict the recorded pressure
head at a selected monitored location one hour ahead. Candidate predictors would include current and lagged tank levels,
flows, actuator states, pressures, and calendar/time features, but not attack labels or any future actuator information. The
hourly sampling interval makes this a different forecasting problem from BattLeDIM and provides a useful comparison of
how temporal resolution affects prediction. I would select the target node after reviewing network topology and pressure
behavior and compare an all-network predictor set with a smaller hydraulically related subset, such as variables from the
same district or from pumps, valves, tanks, and boundary flows that influence the target area. A later error analysis could
separate normal and attack periods; because some BATADAL attacks deliberately falsify SCADA readings, that analysis
would concern the predictability of the recorded signals rather than automatically treating them as true physical pressure.
Sources: Kaggle
| Official BATADAL data
| Taormina et al. (2018), ASCE
Planned evaluation in later stages. For either dataset, I would preserve chronological order when creating training,
validation, and test periods, evaluate forecasts using regression metrics such as MAE and RMSE, and compare learned models
against a simple persistence baseline (future pressure equals current pressure). This provides a clear supervised-learning
framework while avoiding leakage of future information into model training.
Feature scope and dimensionality. The full measurement sets are useful starting points, but I would not assume that
every network variable belongs in the final model. After choosing the target sensor, the EPANET topology can define a
hydraulically related subset—for example nearby pressures, inlet or boundary flows, connected tank levels, relevant pump or
valve states, and local demand information where available. Comparing this physically motivated subset with an all-network
baseline makes feature reduction a testable modeling decision rather than an arbitrary preprocessing step.
Why I am considering these two datasets
Both benchmarks are directly related to pressure behavior in water-distribution systems, contain substantially more observations
than candidate predictors, and include engineering context that can help interpret model inputs and errors. BATADAL fully meets
the stated Kaggle-usability requirement, while BattLeDIM does not; BattLeDIM remains scientifically attractive because of the
quality and reproducibility of its original academic benchmark. Together they frame a clear predictive question for the later stages:
how accurately can future pressure be forecast from information that would actually be available at prediction time?

For either dataset, I would preserve chronological order when creating training,\\r\\nvalidation, and test periods, evaluate forecasts using regression metrics such as MAE and RMSE, and compare learned models\\r\\nagainst a simple persistence baseline (future pressure equals current pressure). This provides a clear supervised-learning\\r\\nframework while avoiding leakage of future information into model training.\\r\\nFeature scope and dimensionality. 1.1 Source, records and suitability
BattLeDIM is an open, simulated drinking-water benchmark representing L-Town, inspired by Cyprus. It was created to compare
leak detection/localization methods. Its continuous time series also allow a supervised pressure-forecasting task. Zenodo supplies
supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) records, leak labels, sensor configuration and EPANET hydraulic models [1,2].
These are simulated observations, not field measurements.
The 2018-2019 release has five-minute spacing: 105,120 timestamps per year, 210,240 total, and 119 numerical measurement
channels per timestamp. The nominal network contains 782 demand junctions, two reservoirs, one tank, 905 pipes, one pump and
three pressure-reducing valves (PRVs). Nominal and generator parameters differ [1].
Quantity
Channels / units
Meaning
Pressure
33; m water head
Pressure at measured junctions.
Pipe/pump flow
3; m3/h
PUMP_1 and p227/p235 flow.
Tank level
1; m
Water level in T1.
AMR demand
82; L/h
Automated meter reading (AMR): consumption assigned to junctions, not pipe
inflow.
Our checks of all eight annual SCADA tables found matching, unique timestamps and no missing values. The benchmark assumes
error-free sensors and no transmission delay/loss. Values are rounded to two decimals; pressure readings average the preceding five
minutes [2], Sensors and Telemetry. Nearby time records are related, so the later evaluation keeps time order. Leak labels are not
model inputs.
1.2 Learning task and hydraulic feature selection
The task is single-output supervised regression: y(u)=p_n1(u+15 min), three samples ahead, in metres. The label is the recorded
five-minute average ending at u+15, including the simulated operating/leak conditions. Each input row is paired with n1 three
timestamps later; there are no target classes. The intended use is a short look-ahead for operators reviewing pressure changes at a
remote junction; operational benefits are not evaluated here. Every input must be available at forecast time u.
Figure 1. Functional supply boundary derived from the released model [1]. Nominal PRV settings are 40/50/35 m; constant settings do not fix
remote-node pressures.
Area C is supplied through T1 and has 92 junctions, three pressure sensors and 82 AMRs. Our nominal-model checks give n1
the longest T1-to-sensor pipe path: 891 m, versus 574/308 m for n4/n31; this distance is not a signal delay. We keep n1/n4/n31
pressure, T1 level, PUMP_1 flow and AMR_n1 demand: 119 channels →87 local/boundary candidates →six signals. T1
represents storage, PUMP_1 measured inflow, n4/n31 the local pressure state, and AMR_n1 consumption at the target junction.
Unlike PRV-3’s single measured pressure, C provides several related measurements. Whether they help n1 forecasting is a question
to test.

 Mini-Project Milestone 2
Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting

Andres Arango Martinez
4/5
3. Your Anticipated Contributions
3.1 Proposed contribution
We propose to forecast recorded n1 pressure 15 minutes ahead using Gradient Boosting and the six signals selected in Section 1:
n1/n4/n31 pressures, T1 level, PUMP_1 flow and AMR_n1 demand. The contribution would be a reproducible comparison for
this specific task using the released SCADA data.
The reviewed graph and adaptive-kNN studies address related prediction tasks. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions
[13,14], and Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees without specifying a future lead [15]. Our review did not identify this
exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. We therefore propose an extension of the reviewed applications, without claiming a new
algorithm or worldwide priority.
Planned contribution
Question to answer
C1. Prepared forecast data
Can we organize past measurements and their +15-minute pressure labels without using future
inputs?
C2. Model comparison
Does Gradient Boosting improve on persistence, Ridge and Random Forest?
C3. Value of extra signals
Do the tank, pump, peer pressures and local demand help beyond n1 history?
3.2 Data preparation and models
We will align timestamps, check units and missing values, and pair each input row with n1 three readings later. Starting inputs will
include current readings and the previous 55 minutes at five-minute intervals, plus time of day and day of week. Only information
available at forecast time will be used; leak labels will stay outside the model inputs. Rows without required values or with a
target outside their assigned period will be excluded. Any scaling for Ridge will be fitted on training data only. Section 1 EDA
motivates these choices but does not establish that every signal helps.
Gradient Boosting adds small decision trees that try to correct earlier prediction errors [18]. We will compare it with persistence (use
the current n1 pressure), Ridge (a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients) [16], and Random Forest (average predictions
from many trees) [17]. These models build on regression and ensemble ideas studied in class. Model settings will be chosen using
validation data; detailed parameter choices are left for implementation.
3.3 Evaluation and expected improvement
We will train on January-September 2018, use October-December 2018 to select settings, then refit on eligible 2018 rows and assess
2019. This preserves time order. Since 2019 has already been inspected, it is a final chronological assessment. All models will
predict the same rows.
We will report MAE (average absolute error) and RMSE (which gives more weight to large errors), both in metres. A boosting
advantage would require lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. To test the extra signals, we will also compare
full boosting with boosting using n1 history and the same calendar variables. Extra-signal value requires lower MAE than this
reduced model and persistence. We will report RMSE tradeoffs and cases where no improvement is found.
The expected outputs are an annotated preparation/model notebook, a comparison table and forecast/error plots. These are
planned experiments; no learning model has been fitted.

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)
